# Video Lessons

The reference shelf for the moment a derivation stalls on a word a video could teach: an integral, a marginal, a gradient, a noise schedule written as code. Each section below holds one short table of timestamped segments for one concept, in the order to watch, tagged intuition, worked example, proof or code walk. It sits beside the [[Textbooks]] shelf, which links the same words to book pages, and belongs to the [[Mathematics]] map.

## How a table is kept here

**This note holds the source.** The text between the two comment lines of a section is the one copy that is edited. The plans and chapters that use the concept carry the same block right after the paragraph where the word first appears, so the table is one click away in the work and one note away for browsing.

**The revision number goes up by one each time a table changes.** The video-pulse skill run with `--sync` compares every copy with its source and brings them level.

**Times are either read from captions or taken from the video's chapter list.** Each table says which in its last line. Captions are automatic, so a caption-read time is good to about 30 seconds, and a chapter-timed row is good to the chapter.

## Substituting a new variable into an integral (u-substitution)

<!-- video-table: u-substitution rev=1 -->
Professor Leonard and the Organic Chemistry Tutor on u-substitution, in the order to watch:

| Order | What it covers | Kind | Watch |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | What substituting u does to an integral, the steps, and how to choose u | Intuition | [Leonard, Calculus 1, 4.2, 0:00 to 6:13](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=aiBD9aI69C8&t=0s) |
| 2 | Transform the integral, solve it in u, then evaluate the new integral | Worked example | [Leonard, Calculus 1, 4.2, 6:13 to 14:07](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=aiBD9aI69C8&t=373s) |
| 3 | An integrand of the form e to a function of x | Worked example | [Organic Chemistry Tutor, U-Substitution, 3:53 to 5:47](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=sdYdnpYn-1o&t=233s) |
| 4 | A plain linear substitution, the shift u = x minus a | Worked example | [Organic Chemistry Tutor, U-Substitution, 11:39 to 13:38](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=sdYdnpYn-1o&t=699s) |
| 5 | A definite integral, changing the bounds into u so no back-substitution is needed | Worked example | [Organic Chemistry Tutor, U-Substitution With Definite Integrals, 0:00 to 6:48](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=tM4RWc9ryx0&t=0s) |

No video found proves the rule as the chain rule run backwards, and none works a substitution inside a double integral. Times are chapter boundaries read from each video's chapter list, not from captions, so each is good to the chapter and the sentence inside it is not checked.
<!-- /video-table -->

## Area under a curve, and the integral over the whole line

<!-- video-table: area-and-whole-line-integrals rev=1 -->
Professor Leonard on the integral in that last check (area under a curve, then the integral over the whole line), in the order to watch:

| Order | What it covers | Kind | Watch |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Area under a curve as a limit of rectangles, and why the sum becomes an integral | Definition | [Calculus 1, 4.3, 22:51 to 53:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=F0uuW-I6icY&t=1371s) |
| 2 | Area under x² on [0, 1] as a limit, which comes to 1/3 | Worked example | [Calculus 1, 4.3, 1:05:50 to 1:22:30](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=F0uuW-I6icY&t=3950s) |
| 3 | Plug the bounds into the antiderivative, with no +C | Definition | [Calculus 1, 4.5, 0:01 to 8:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=xjtEfS0vY2o&t=1s) |
| 4 | Why an infinite bound makes an integral improper | Definition | [Calculus 2, 7.6, 0:00 to 7:30](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=g-M8FHslgdk&t=1s) |
| 5 | Replace the infinity by b, take a limit, and read convergent or divergent | Definition | [Calculus 2, 7.6, 15:00 to 24:30](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=g-M8FHslgdk&t=900s) |
| 6 | An integral over (−∞, ∞): split at a point and take two limits | Worked example | [Calculus 2, 7.6, 1:19:30 to 1:34:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=g-M8FHslgdk&t=4770s) |

Leonard has no video on a density integrating to 1 or on the Gaussian integral itself. Timestamps come from his auto-generated captions and are good to about 30 seconds.
<!-- /video-table -->

## Integration by parts

<!-- video-table: integration-by-parts rev=2 -->
Professor Leonard on integration by parts, in the order to watch:

| Order | What it covers | Kind | Watch |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | The formula, derived by integrating the product rule | Proof | [Calculus 2, 7.1, 0:31 to 8:15](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=EOwjiFpDY_s&t=31s) |
| 2 | Choosing u and v, then x eˣ worked through | Worked example | [Calculus 2, 7.1, 8:27 to 17:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=EOwjiFpDY_s&t=507s) |
| 3 | ln x from 1 to e, with the uv term evaluated at the bounds | Worked example | [Calculus 2, 7.1, 58:45 to 1:05:30](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=EOwjiFpDY_s&t=3525s) |
| 4 | Parts twice, until the integral comes back and can be solved for | Worked example | [Calculus 2, 7.1, 46:00 to 50:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=EOwjiFpDY_s&t=2760s) |
| 5 | The uv term vanishing at infinity: x eˣ from −∞ to 0. This is the boundary assumption in integration by parts | Worked example | [Calculus 2, 7.6, 1:00:00 to 1:17:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=g-M8FHslgdk&t=3600s) |

These are single-variable. The score-matching derivation applies the same idea in each coordinate with the others held fixed, which Leonard does not cover. Timestamps are good to about 30 seconds.
<!-- /video-table -->

## When an integral to infinity diverges

<!-- video-table: diverging-integrals rev=1 -->
Professor Leonard on when an integral to infinity is finite and when it diverges, in the order to watch:

| Order | What it covers | Kind | Watch |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Replace the infinity by b, take a limit, and read convergent or divergent | Definition | [Calculus 2, 7.6, 15:00 to 24:30](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=g-M8FHslgdk&t=900s) |
| 2 | ∫ 1/xⁿ from 1 to ∞ converges for n above 1 and diverges for n at most 1 | Proof | [Calculus 2, 7.6, 31:20 to 43:30](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=g-M8FHslgdk&t=1880s) |
| 3 | An integral over (−∞, ∞): split at a point and take two limits | Worked example | [Calculus 2, 7.6, 1:19:30 to 1:34:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=g-M8FHslgdk&t=4770s) |

Leonard does not cover e^(−x²) converging against e^(+x²) diverging. Row 2 runs the same limit test on a simpler function. Timestamps are good to about 30 seconds.
<!-- /video-table -->

| Order | What it covers | Kind | Watch |

## The product of two Gaussians and the precision-weighted mean

<!-- video-table: product-of-two-gaussians rev=1 -->
Michel van Biezen, then Robert Ghrist from the open web, on multiplying two Gaussians, in the order to watch:

| Order | What it covers | Kind | Watch |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | The gain is the estimate's error over the sum of both errors, and the new value moves toward the reading by that share, so the surer of the two pulls harder | Intuition | [Van Biezen, Kalman Filter 3 of 55, 0:28 to 3:51](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=fpRb1sjFz_M&t=28s) |
| 2 | One update with numbers: an estimate of 68 with error 2 and a reading of 75 with error 4 give 70.33 with error 1.33 | Worked example | [Van Biezen, Kalman Filter 6 of 55, 0:13 to 2:14](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=SIQJaqYVtuE&t=13s) |
| 3 | The product of two one-dimensional Gaussians is a scaled Gaussian, found by simplifying the quadratic in the exponent | Proof, open web | [Ghrist, CalcBLUE 3, 18.4, 0:26 to 4:10](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=lUORBdNccHc&t=26s) |
| 4 | The same product in several dimensions | Proof, open web | [Ghrist, CalcBLUE 3, 18.4, 4:10 to 5:53](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=lUORBdNccHc&t=250s) |

Van Biezen works the product as a Kalman filter update and calls the two variances errors: his old value plus the gain times the difference is the precision-weighted mean rearranged, and his new error of 1.33 from 2 and 4 is the two precisions added. No listed creator derives the product of two Gaussian densities, so rows 3 and 4 come from the open web, and no row was found that computes the overlap Z. Rows 1 and 2 are read from automatic captions and good to about 30 seconds; rows 3 and 4 are chapter boundaries, so the sentence inside each is not checked.
<!-- /video-table -->

## The arithmetic mean is at least the geometric mean (two numbers)

<!-- video-table: am-gm-two-numbers rev=1 -->
MathOdyssey, then David Metzler, both from the open web, on the proof that (a + b)/2 is at least the square root of ab, in the order to watch:

| Order | What it covers | Kind | Watch |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Subtract the geometric mean from the arithmetic mean, write a and b as squares of their roots, and the difference becomes (root a minus root b) squared over 2, which cannot be negative; equality only when a equals b | Proof, open web | [MathOdyssey, Proof of AM-GM for two positive numbers, 0:16 to 3:05](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=8sMtm9JfMfI&t=16s) |
| 2 | Square both sides to find a hint, reach (a minus b) squared at least 0, then run the steps forwards as the proof | Proof, open web | [Metzler, Proof example: AM-GM Inequality, 4:35 to 7:58](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=MVSmvLyowt0&t=275s) |

No listed creator proves the inequality, and no worked example with numbers was found. Metzler also shows how to find the proof (1:20 to 4:35) and proves that square roots keep an inequality (8:13 to 9:51). Times are read from automatic captions and good to about 30 seconds.
<!-- /video-table -->

## The correlation coefficient

<!-- video-table: correlation-coefficient rev=2 -->
Mahesh Huddar, then StatQuest, then DataMListic, on Pearson's correlation (covariance over the two standard deviations), in the order to watch:

| Order | What it covers | Kind | Watch |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Correlation runs from minus 1 to 1: perfect positive, perfect negative, and no linear relationship at 0 | Intuition | [Mahesh Huddar, How to find the coefficient of correlation, 0:31 to 2:23](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=bXlYuKFGsZY&t=31s) |
| 2 | Four data points: the two means, the covariance, both variances, and r = covariance over root variance times root variance, which comes out 0 | Worked example | [Mahesh Huddar, How to find the coefficient of correlation, 2:23 to 6:10](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=bXlYuKFGsZY&t=143s) |
| 3 | The denominator squeezes covariance into minus 1 to 1; covariance 116 with variances 101.8 and 160.3 gives 0.9 | Worked example | [StatQuest, Pearson's Correlation, Clearly Explained, 13:37 to 16:04](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=xZ_z8KWkhXE&t=817s) |
| 4 | Why divide at all: covariance gives only the direction of a relationship and changes when weight is measured in grams instead of kilograms, while correlation also gives its strength and does not change | Intuition | [DataMListic, Covariance vs correlation, 0:00 to 0:53](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=PzOijddw4eg&t=0s) |

Mahesh Huddar's example ends at 0, so it shows the arithmetic but not a strong relationship; StatQuest supplies the nonzero answer and assumes his variance and covariance videos; DataMListic's one-minute short names the unit change without working it. None of the three proves that r stays between minus 1 and 1. Times are read from automatic captions, good to about 30 seconds for the first three rows and to a few seconds for the short.
<!-- /video-table -->

## The phi and Matthews correlation coefficient (a 2 by 2 table)

<!-- video-table: phi-matthews-correlation rev=1 -->
Pedram Jahangiry, from the open web, on the Matthews correlation coefficient of a confusion matrix, in the order to watch:

| Order | What it covers | Kind | Watch |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | The coefficient is the correlation between actual and predicted: true positive times true negative minus false positive times false negative, over the square root of the four pair sums; it runs from minus 1 to 1 | Intuition, open web | [Jahangiry, Performance metrics for classification models, 16:22 to 18:24](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=IDPmX6xT3Us&t=982s) |
| 2 | 100 cases with 2 defaulters and a model that always says no default: accuracy 98 percent, precision, recall and F1 all 0, and the coefficient read as 0 | Worked example, open web | [Jahangiry, Performance metrics for classification models, 18:54 to 20:30](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=IDPmX6xT3Us&t=1134s) |

Nothing on Mahesh Huddar's channel covers the phi or Matthews coefficient, and no video on a listed creator does. The example skips the arithmetic of the denominator: with that table it is 0, so the coefficient is undefined and conventionally set to 0. He does not name the phi coefficient, which is the same quantity for any two yes-or-no columns. Times are read from automatic captions and good to about 30 seconds.
<!-- /video-table -->

## The bootstrap

<!-- video-table: bootstrap-resampling rev=1 -->
StatQuest, then Joshua French from the open web, on the bootstrap (resampling a data set with replacement to get a standard error or an interval), in the order to watch:

| Order | What it covers | Kind | Watch |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Eight patients with a mean response of 0.5, and why repeating the whole experiment to see how far that mean moves is too costly | Intuition | [StatQuest, Bootstrapping Main Ideas, 0:17 to 2:18](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Xz0x-8-cgaQ&t=17s) |
| 2 | Draw from the eight values eight times with replacement, take the mean, repeat thousands of times; the four steps and the name for sampling with replacement | Worked example | [StatQuest, Bootstrapping Main Ideas, 2:18 to 5:22](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Xz0x-8-cgaQ&t=138s) |
| 3 | The standard error is the standard deviation of the bootstrap means; a 95 percent interval is the stretch that covers 95 percent of them | Definition | [StatQuest, Bootstrapping Main Ideas, 6:30 to 7:02](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Xz0x-8-cgaQ&t=390s) |
| 4 | A sample of numbers, 1000 resamples in R, the 2.5 and 97.5 percent quantiles of the resample means, and the interval 2.44 to 4.98 for a true mean of 3 | Worked example, code walk, open web | [Joshua French, Bootstrap confidence interval example, 2:12 to 6:12](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=IBuJSRu9Quw&t=132s) |

StatQuest shows the eight values on screen without reading them out and gives no number for the standard error. French reaches an interval, not a standard error, and uses the percentile interval only. Neither covers when the bootstrap fails. Mahesh Huddar's channel has no video on it. Times are read from automatic captions and good to about 30 seconds.
<!-- /video-table -->

## Solving an inequality with the unknown in the exponent

<!-- video-table: exponent-inequality-with-logarithms rev=1 -->
Hasegi Inc., then TLMaths, both from the open web, on solving an inequality such as 0.5^x < 10^300 with logarithms, in the order to watch:

| Order | What it covers | Kind | Watch |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | (1/2)^x < (1/2)^3 gives x > 3: a base below 1 makes the function fall, so the order reverses | Intuition, open web | [Hasegi Inc., Why an Exponential Inequality Flips When the Base Is Below 1, 0:03 to 1:32](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=WzR5E7dGM7M&t=3s) |
| 2 | Write 1/2 as 2^(-1), compare exponents to get -x < -3, and multiplying both sides by -1 flips the sign: the same flip as dividing by a negative | Intuition, open web | [Hasegi Inc., Why an Exponential Inequality Flips When the Base Is Below 1, 1:32 to 2:05](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=WzR5E7dGM7M&t=92s) |
| 3 | Solve 0.5^x < 10^300: take natural logs of both sides, bring both exponents down, divide by ln 0.5 = -0.693, which is negative, so the sign reverses and x > -996.57 | Worked example, open web | [TLMaths, Solving 0.5^x less than 10^300, 0:06 to 2:23](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=nDr4CrZ_CO8&t=6s) |
| 4 | Why writing it as a log with base 0.5 in one step goes wrong (the flip is forgotten), and why to go through natural logs | Worked example, open web | [TLMaths, Solving 0.5^x less than 10^300, 2:23 to 3:27](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=nDr4CrZ_CO8&t=143s) |

Neither video solves for a whole number of repeats or rounds the answer up, and neither starts from a base above 1 with a positive log. TLMaths refers to an earlier video of his for the base 2 case. Professor Leonard's logarithm lecture (Precalculus 64) was checked by chapter titles only, which name equations, so it is not linked. Times are read from automatic captions and good to about 30 seconds.
<!-- /video-table -->

## Inverse transform sampling worked on the exponential

<!-- video-table: inverse-transform-exponential-example rev=1 -->
Susu Zhang, then Marcus Pendergrass, both from the open web, on turning uniform random numbers into exponential ones with the inverse CDF, in the order to watch:

| Order | What it covers | Kind | Watch |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | From the exponential density to its CDF 1 - e^(-λx), set equal to u, and solved for x = -(1/λ) ln(1 - u) | Worked example, open web | [Susu Zhang, Lec1 2 2 Example Exp, 0:06 to 4:22](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=joBKfHH5bwc&t=6s) |
| 2 | The inverse as an R function, 1000 uniform draws, the transform with λ = 1, and a histogram laid over the exponential density | Code walk, open web | [Susu Zhang, Lec1 2 2 Example Exp, 4:22 to 10:22](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=joBKfHH5bwc&t=262s) |
| 3 | 10,000 uniforms in Desmos, -ln(1 - u) for λ = 1, then multiplied by 2 to get λ = 1/2 | Worked example, open web | [Marcus Pendergrass, Using The Inverse CDF Method to Simulate Random Variables, 3:24 to 6:36](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=xf-ltpf7rwQ&t=204s) |

Both work by simulating many draws and comparing to the density; neither turns two or three specific uniform numbers into exponential values by hand, and neither proves the transformed draws have the target CDF. Pendergrass cites the two facts he uses to his own course notes. The ritvikmath block above covers the CDF and the inverse. Mahesh Huddar's channel has no video on it. Times are read from automatic captions and good to about 30 seconds.
<!-- /video-table -->

## The noise prediction is the score, rescaled

<!-- video-table: noise-prediction-is-the-score rev=1 -->
Vizuara, then Deepia from the open web, on why a network that predicts the added noise is predicting the score, in the order to watch:

| Order | What it covers | Kind | Code it writes | Watch |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Noise a clean point with a Gaussian of standard deviation σ, take the log of that Gaussian and its gradient: the score of the noised point given the clean one is −(x̃ − x)/σ² | Proof | | [Vizuara, Principles of Diffusion Models, lecture 6, 32:45 to 38:19](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6ELzfPXUqps&t=1965s) |
| 2 | That score points from the noised point back to the clean one, so it is the added noise, which is what a DDPM network is trained to predict | Intuition | | [Vizuara, Principles of Diffusion Models, lecture 6, 38:19 to 40:25](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6ELzfPXUqps&t=2299s) |
| 3 | The training loss in code, with the target score written as −(x − x̃)/σ² and reduced to −ε/σ | Code walk | the loss whose target is `-noise / sigma` | [Vizuara, Principles of Diffusion Models, lecture 6, 42:54 to 44:49](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6ELzfPXUqps&t=2574s) |
| 4 | The denoiser that minimises squared error returns the average clean point given the noisy one | Intuition, open web | | [Deepia, Unadjusted Langevin Algorithm, 1:34 to 5:43](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=m0OTso2Dc2U&t=94s) |
| 5 | Tweedie's formula: that average gives the score of the whole noised distribution, the step from one point's score to the marginal score | Proof, open web | | [Deepia, Unadjusted Langevin Algorithm, 5:43 to 10:23](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=m0OTso2Dc2U&t=343s) |

Vizuara noises with x̃ = x + σε and no shrinking of x, so the factor is 1/σ where a DDPM schedule has 1/√(1 − ᾱ_t); no listed creator covers the marginal step, so rows 4 and 5 come from Deepia on the open web. Rows 1 to 3 are read from automatic captions and good to about 30 seconds; rows 4 and 5 are chapter boundaries from the video's chapter list, so the sentence inside each is not checked.
<!-- /video-table -->

## Score matching

<!-- video-table: score-matching rev=1 -->
Stanford CS236, then Vizuara, on score matching, the loss that fits a model's score using only samples of the data, in the order to watch:

| Order | What it covers | Kind | Watch |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | The Fisher divergence, the squared distance between the model's score and the data's score | Intuition | [Stanford CS236, lecture 12, 34:37 to 38:02](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Nci1Bepcy0g&t=2077s) |
| 2 | Integration by parts removes the unknown data score from that loss | Proof | [Stanford CS236, lecture 12, 38:02 to 46:28](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Nci1Bepcy0g&t=2282s) |
| 3 | The score matching objective that results, which needs only samples | Intuition | [Stanford CS236, lecture 12, 46:28 to 50:36](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Nci1Bepcy0g&t=2788s) |
| 4 | What the two terms of that objective do: the trace pulls nearby points in toward the data, the norm makes the score zero on the data | Intuition | [Vizuara, Principles of Diffusion Models, lecture 6, 5:20 to 9:23](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6ELzfPXUqps&t=320s) |
| 5 | Why it is not used on images: the trace needs the whole D by D matrix, so its cost grows as D², 784² for a 28 by 28 image | Intuition | [Vizuara, Principles of Diffusion Models, lecture 6, 9:23 to 12:43](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6ELzfPXUqps&t=563s) |

Neither works score matching on numbers, and the denoising version that diffusion training uses is its own table. Rows 1 to 3 are chapter boundaries from CS236's chapter list, so the sentence inside each is not checked; rows 4 and 5 are read from Vizuara's automatic captions and good to about 30 seconds.

## Total variation distance

<!-- video-table: total-variation-distance rev=1 -->
Mary Wootters, from the open web, on the total variation distance between two distributions, in the order to watch:

| Order | What it covers | Kind | Watch |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | TV is half the sum of the absolute differences between the two probability tables, which equals the largest difference in probability the two give any one event | Intuition, open web | [Wootters, Total Variation Distance, 0:16 to 1:35](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=aJP0Bj6jVkI&t=16s) |
| 2 | Two bar charts split where each is the higher: the two shaded areas are equal, and each one is the TV | Proof, open web | [Wootters, Total Variation Distance, 1:35 to 3:47](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=aJP0Bj6jVkI&t=95s) |
| 3 | A fair coin against one that lands heads 0.6 of the time: TV = ½(0.1 + 0.1) = 0.1 | Worked example, open web | [Wootters, Total Variation Distance, 4:34 to 6:42](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=aJP0Bj6jVkI&t=274s) |

No listed creator covers it. Wootters compares two known tables; she does not estimate TV from two sets of samples on a binning, so the sampling-noise baseline that two draws of the same data reach is not covered. Times are read from automatic captions and good to about 30 seconds.
<!-- /video-table -->

## Scaled dot-product self-attention

<!-- video-table: scaled-dot-product-self-attention rev=2 -->
3Blue1Brown, then Stanford CS231N, on the attention operator, in the order to watch:

| Order | What it covers | Kind | Watch |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Orient, before you compare vectors by angle: the same word needs a different vector in each context ("mole" in three phrases, "tower" after "Eiffel" and then "miniature"). Watch for the job a lookup with no context cannot do | Intuition | [3Blue1Brown, Deep Learning chapter 6, 0:00 to 4:29](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=eMlx5fFNoYc&t=0s) |
| 2 | Orient, before you compute a query, a key and a value by hand: one head of attention set up on "a fluffy blue creature", each word starting as an embedding e, with the work done as matrix-vector products of learned weights | Intuition | [3Blue1Brown, Deep Learning chapter 6, 4:29 to 6:14](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=eMlx5fFNoYc&t=269s) |
| 3 | Confirm, after you compute a query and step W_Q: the query is the question a word asks, made by multiplying W_Q by its embedding, one query per word. Watch for this being what a projection means | Intuition | [3Blue1Brown, Deep Learning chapter 6, 6:14 to 7:47](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=eMlx5fFNoYc&t=374s) |
| 4 | Confirm, after you separate keys from values: the key matrix gives each word a key, and a key answers a query when the two align | Intuition | [3Blue1Brown, Deep Learning chapter 6, 7:47 to 8:27](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=eMlx5fFNoYc&t=467s) |
| 5 | Confirm, after you turn scores into weights and blend: the grid of every key-query dot product, then a softmax down each column so each column sums to 1. Watch for the grid being named the attention pattern | Intuition | [3Blue1Brown, Deep Learning chapter 6, 8:27 to 10:18](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=eMlx5fFNoYc&t=507s) |
| 6 | Confirm, after you return the blend of the values: the value matrix gives each word the vector it would add to another, each value is scaled by its weight in the column, and the sum is added to the embedding | Intuition | [3Blue1Brown, Deep Learning chapter 6, 13:10 to 15:59](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=eMlx5fFNoYc&t=790s) |
| 7 | Confirm after the sitting: why the dot product is scaled by the square root of the width | Intuition | [CS231N, lecture 8, 32:20 to 34:20](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=RQowiOF_FvQ&t=1940s) |
| 8 | Confirm after the sitting: project each input to a key and a value, compare every key with every query, softmax per query, and combine the values | Proof | [CS231N, lecture 8, 38:40 to 44:50](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=RQowiOF_FvQ&t=2320s) |

## The Gaussian density and its formula

<!-- video-table: gaussian-density rev=1 -->
StatQuest, then 3Blue1Brown, on the Gaussian density and why its formula has the pieces it has, in the order to watch:

| Order | What it covers | Kind | Watch |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | A curve as a distribution: probability is the area under it over an interval, not its height at a point | Intuition | [StatQuest, Main Ideas behind Probability Distributions, 2:15 to 5:14](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=oI3hZJqXJuc&t=135s) |
| 2 | The Gaussian formula unpacked one piece at a time | Intuition | [3Blue1Brown, But what is the Central Limit Theorem?, 15:54 to 20:47](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=zeJD6dqJ5lo&t=954s) |
| 3 | Stripped of its constants the bell is e^(−x²), whose area is √π, so dividing by √π makes the area 1 | Intuition | [3Blue1Brown, Why π is in the normal distribution, 2:07 to 2:59](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=cy8r7WSuT1I&t=127s) |
| 4 | Two demands on a density in the plane, that it depend only on distance from the centre and that x and y be independent, which make it the product g(x)·g(y) | Intuition | [3Blue1Brown, Why π is in the normal distribution, 13:15 to 15:18](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=cy8r7WSuT1I&t=795s) |
| 5 | Those two demands force the shape e^(c·r²) with c negative, and a constant in front then rescales the volume to 1 | Proof | [3Blue1Brown, Why π is in the normal distribution, 15:18 to 21:34](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=cy8r7WSuT1I&t=918s) |

No video found evaluates the density's height at a point, as the toy's 0.7979 and 0.4839 do, or shows a height above 1, as the bar's 1.25 is. Rows 3 to 5 are read from 3Blue1Brown's captions and good to about 30 seconds; rows 1 and 2 are chapter boundaries from each video's chapter list, so the sentence inside each is not checked.
<!-- /video-table -->

## Where the denoising loss comes from

Stanford CME296, then Stanford CS236, on where the denoising loss comes from, in the order to watch:

| Order | What it covers | Kind | Watch |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | The KL divergence between two Gaussians, the true reverse step and the learned one, reduces to the squared distance between the predicted noise and the noise that was added | Proof | [CME296, lecture 1, 1:18:03 to 1:21:03](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=tr-CUpw--ck&t=4683s) |
| 2 | The loss read term by term: the sampled step t, the clean image x_0, the noise ε, and the network ε_θ given the noised image √ᾱ_t·x_0 + √(1 − ᾱ_t)·ε together with t | Intuition | [CME296, lecture 1, 1:21:12 to 1:24:04](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=tr-CUpw--ck&t=4872s) |
| 3 | The exact KL puts a weight on each step's error, and DDPM sets every weight to 1 | Intuition | [CME296, lecture 1, 1:26:17 to 1:27:03](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=tr-CUpw--ck&t=5177s) |
| 4 | Predicting the noise shown to be the same as denoising score matching | Proof | [Stanford CS236, lecture 16, 39:01 to 41:48](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=VsllsC2JMGY&t=2341s) |

## The exponential moving average

<!-- video-table: exponential-moving-average rev=1 -->
Andrew Ng (DeepLearning.AI, from the open web) on the exponentially weighted average, in the order to watch:

| Order | What it covers | Kind | Code it writes | Watch |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | London's daily temperatures smoothed with v = 0.9·v + 0.1·θ, one day at a time | Worked example, open web | | [Ng, Exponentially Weighted Averages, 0:21 to 2:36](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=lAq96T8FkTw&t=21s) |
| 2 | The average covers roughly 1/(1 − β) recent values: β = 0.98 is smoother and lags, β = 0.5 is noisy and quick | Intuition, open web | | [Ng, Exponentially Weighted Averages, 2:36 to 5:21](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=lAq96T8FkTw&t=156s) |
| 3 | Unrolling the update: the value k steps back carries weight 0.1 × 0.9^k, so the weights fall exponentially with age | Proof, open web | | [Ng, Understanding Exponentially Weighted Averages, 0:41 to 4:36](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=NxTFlzBjS-4&t=41s) |
| 4 | 0.9 to the power 10 is about 1/e, so an entry's weight falls to about a third after 1/(1 − β) steps | Intuition, open web | | [Ng, Understanding Exponentially Weighted Averages, 4:36 to 6:50](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=NxTFlzBjS-4&t=276s) |
| 5 | The update as one line inside a loop, keeping a single number in memory | Code walk, open web | `v = beta * v + (1 - beta) * theta` | [Ng, Understanding Exponentially Weighted Averages, 6:50 to 9:25](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=NxTFlzBjS-4&t=410s) |

No listed creator has a video on it. Ng averages temperatures with β = 0.9, not model weights with 0.999, and he measures the fade by 1/e, not by the half-life of 692.8 steps. Times are read from automatic captions and good to about 30 seconds.
<!-- /video-table -->

## Total variation distance as a distance

<!-- video-table: total-variation-distance-properties rev=1 -->
Philippe Rigollet (MIT 18.650, from the open web) on total variation distance as a true distance between distributions, in the order to watch:

| Order | What it covers | Kind | Watch |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | TV is the largest gap the two distributions give any one event; a TV of 0.01 puts every probability within 0.01 of the true one | Intuition, open web | [Rigollet, MIT 18.650 lecture 4, 35:35 to 40:18](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=rLlZpnT02ZU&t=2135s) |
| 2 | For densities, TV is half the area between the two curves, drawn for two Gaussians | Intuition, open web | [Rigollet, MIT 18.650 lecture 4, 45:54 to 48:37](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=rLlZpnT02ZU&t=2754s) |
| 3 | It is a distance: symmetric, never negative, zero only when the two distributions agree, and it obeys the triangle inequality | Proof, open web | [Rigollet, MIT 18.650 lecture 4, 48:41 to 51:37](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=rLlZpnT02ZU&t=2921s) |
| 4 | Half the summed gap equals the largest event gap, which is reached on the set where one density is above the other | Proof, open web | [Rigollet, MIT 18.650 lecture 4, 51:49 to 1:00:17](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=rLlZpnT02ZU&t=3109s) |
| 5 | Why TV is hard to estimate from samples: it is not an average of anything over the data, which is why maximum likelihood turns to KL instead | Intuition, open web | [Rigollet, MIT 18.650 lecture 4, 1:02:26 to 1:05:03](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=rLlZpnT02ZU&t=3746s) |

No listed creator covers it. Rigollet never bins two sample sets, so the sampling-noise baseline still has no video, and his only number is the 0.01 bound; Wootters' coin in the earlier total variation table is the worked example. Times are read from automatic captions and good to about 30 seconds.
<!-- /video-table -->

## Sampling temperature

<!-- video-table: sampling-temperature rev=2 -->
CME295, then Vizuara in code, then 3Blue1Brown's picture of it, on sampling temperature, in the order to watch:

| Order | What it covers and what to watch for | Kind | When | Code it writes | Watch |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Temperature divides every score before the softmax: low makes the distribution spiky, high makes it flat. Watch for which way your own setting pushes. | Intuition | Orient, before the first run | none | [CME295 lecture 3, 52:20 to 55:40](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Q5baLehv5So&t=3140s) |
| 2 | The limit worked on the board: as temperature goes to 0 all probability lands on the top token, and as it grows the distribution goes uniform. | Proof | Confirm, after your counts | none | [CME295 lecture 3, 55:40 to 1:00:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Q5baLehv5So&t=3340s) |
| 3 | Any temperature above 0 gives a different output each run, and even 0 can differ because the hardware sums numbers in a different order. Watch for why temperature 0 is near-deterministic, not deterministic. | Intuition | Confirm, after your counts | none | [CME295 lecture 3, 1:02:20 to 1:04:40](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Q5baLehv5So&t=3740s) |
| 4 | The same idea in code: logits divided by the temperature, a softmax, and the next token drawn with a multinomial 1,000 times, the likeliest coming up 582 times. Watch for the count, which is your distinct-answer count in miniature. | Code walk | Unblock, if your counts look wrong | the temperature-scaled softmax and `torch.multinomial` | [Vizuara, top-k sampling, 2:00 to 5:20](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=EhU32O7DkA4&t=120s) |
| 5 | The same softmax drawn as bars over the next word: at temperature 0 the largest score always wins, and higher temperatures give the less likely words more weight. Watch for the bars flattening as T grows. | Intuition | Confirm, after your counts | none | [3Blue1Brown, Deep Learning Chapter 5, 23:55 to 25:33](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=wjZofJX0v4M&t=1435s) |

| 3 | γ, the probability that one fish came from class k given its measurements, written with Bayes' theorem as π_k times the class-k normal, divided by the same sum over every class | Proof | [ritvikmath, Gaussian Mixture Model, 8:47 to 10:13](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=EWd1xRkyEog&t=527s) |

Neither uses the shares to weight score arrows, as the mixture score here does, so the toy's (0.3609, −0.4392) against the plain sum's (1.3192, −0.1786) has no video. Rows 1 and 2 are chapter boundaries from Serrano's chapter list, so the sentence inside each is not checked; row 3 is read from ritvikmath's automatic captions and good to about 30 seconds.

| 2 | The expected returns on property and on shares behind the rule | Proof or rule, open web | [Ben Felix, Renting vs. Buying a Home: The 5% Rule, 3:37 to 6:46](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Uwl3-jBNEd4&t=217s) |
| 3 | The unrecoverable costs of owning set against rent, worked | Worked example, open web | [Ben Felix, Renting vs. Buying a Home: The 5% Rule, 6:46 to 8:26](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Uwl3-jBNEd4&t=406s) |
| 4 | Opportunity cost: what the deposit would have earned invested | Intuition, open web | [Ben Felix, Renting vs. Buying a Home: The 5% Rule, 8:26 to 9:21](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Uwl3-jBNEd4&t=506s) |
| 5 | The total cost of ownership, then the financial indifference model | Proof, open web | [Ben Felix, Renting vs. Buying: What People Get Wrong, 3:41 to 9:12](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=j4H9LL7A-nQ&t=221s) |
| 6 | A Cape Town property bought against rented, worked in rand | Worked example, open web | [Money with Carla, Buy or Rent in Cape Town? The Rule of 8.5%, 4:06 to 11:23](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=wPT7fFpNL7I&t=246s) |
| 7 | Her South African version of the rule, 8.5% | Proof or rule, open web | [Money with Carla, Buy or Rent in Cape Town? The Rule of 8.5%, 20:07 to 23:20](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=wPT7fFpNL7I&t=1207s) |
| 8 | The year buying overtakes renting in a R3m comparison | Worked example, open web | [Rowan Alexander, Buying or Renting: Which Builds More Wealth?, 7:33 to 8:05](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=5hZSG0kxzQQ&t=453s) |

Every one assumes the buyer pays rent today, so the comparison carries over only through the unrecoverable costs of owning and the opportunity cost of the deposit, rows 1, 3 and 4; for someone who pays no rent the rent side is zero. Ben Felix uses Canadian figures and runs a portfolio management firm; Money with Carla sells a calculator and a course; Rowan Alexander points viewers to his bond originator. Times are chapter boundaries read from each video's chapter list, not from captions, so each is good to the chapter and the sentence inside it is not checked.

Financially Fabulous Females is a commission-earning adviser; Money Marx sells a paid course. Rows 7 to 9 are chapter boundaries from each video's chapter list, so the sentence inside each is not checked; the rest are read from automatic captions and good to about 30 seconds.

## The t-test against the z-test

<!-- video-table: t-test-against-z-test rev=1 -->
DataMListic on choosing between a t-test and a z-test, in the order to watch:

| Order | What it covers | Kind | Watch |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | The rule: a known population standard deviation means a z-test on the normal curve; an unknown one means a t-test, which uses the sample standard deviation and the t distribution's heavier tails | Intuition | [DataMListic, T-Test vs Z-Test, 0:00 to 0:25](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=1Gi4clGjPeU&t=0s) |
| 2 | Why "large sample, so use z" is the wrong rule: with the population standard deviation unknown, the t-test applies at any sample size | Intuition | [DataMListic, T-Test vs Z-Test, 0:25 to 0:47](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=1Gi4clGjPeU&t=25s) |

The short tests a mean and works no numbers. It never covers a proportion, where the null hypothesis itself fixes the standard deviation at the square root of p(1 − p), which is why the pooled two-proportion test reads its p-value off the normal curve. Times are read from the automatic captions of a 55-second short, so each start is good to a few seconds.
<!-- /video-table -->

## Least squares as maximum likelihood

<!-- video-table: least-squares-as-maximum-likelihood rev=1 -->
DataMListic, then Stanford CS229, on why fitting by least squares is maximum likelihood under Gaussian noise, in the order to watch:

| Order | What it covers | Kind | Watch |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | The two pieces: least squares picks the intercept and slope that minimise the summed squared differences, and a Gaussian is set by its mean and its standard deviation | Intuition | [DataMListic, Linear Regression vs Maximum Likelihood, 0:00 to 0:36](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=0oqWzkgfq7w&t=0s) |
| 2 | The claim: least squares is the same as placing a Gaussian on each point of the regression line and maximising the likelihood | Intuition | [DataMListic, Linear Regression vs Maximum Likelihood, 0:36 to 0:47](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=0oqWzkgfq7w&t=36s) |
| 3 | The proof: the log of the Gaussian likelihood is a constant minus the summed squared errors over 2σ², so maximising it minimises the least squares cost | Proof | [Stanford CS229, Spring 2026, lecture 3, 27:13 to 30:13](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=uJF_gL3jhxI&t=1633s) |

DataMListic states the result and leaves the derivation to a longer video; CS229's row is that derivation. Neither turns a KL between two Gaussians of equal variance into a squared distance between their means, which is the same algebra applied to a second Gaussian. DataMListic's times are read from the automatic captions of a 54-second short and are good to a few seconds; CS229's are read from the lecture's automatic captions and are good to about 30 seconds.
<!-- /video-table -->

## The variance of a sum of independent variables, and of a scaled variable

<!-- video-table: variance-of-a-sum-of-independent-variables rev=1 -->
ritvikmath, then three lessons from the open web (MIT OpenCourseWare, Snell Bros. Math, Khan Academy), on the variance of a sum of independent variables and of a variable multiplied by a constant, in the order to watch:

| Order | What it covers | Kind | Watch |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | The mean of a sum always splits into a sum of means; the variance splits only when the variables are independent | Intuition | [ritvikmath, The Binomial Distribution, 9:23 to 10:09](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=oLnmWcbYnrM&t=563s) |
| 2 | Var(X + Y) = Var(X) + Var(Y) for independent X and Y: expand the square, and independence sends E[XY] to zero | Proof, open web | [MIT OpenCourseWare, L07.7 Independence and Variances, 0:33 to 2:43](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=YQ26hzI4OJk&t=33s) |
| 3 | Var(aX + b) = a² Var(X), expanded term by term | Proof, open web | [Snell Bros. Math, Var(aX + b) = a²Var(X), 0:00 to 3:31](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=JOCOW8MQB_4&t=0s) |
| 4 | When independence fails: X = Y gives 4 Var(X) and X = −Y gives 0; with X independent of Y, Var(X − 3Y) = Var(X) + 9 Var(Y) | Worked example, open web | [MIT OpenCourseWare, L07.7 Independence and Variances, 2:43 to 5:02](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=YQ26hzI4OJk&t=163s) |
| 5 | Standard deviations 0.8 and 0.6 ounces: the variances 0.64 and 0.36 add to 1, while the standard deviations do not add | Worked example, open web | [Khan Academy, Variance of sum and difference of random variables, 3:08 to 7:56](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=3VXhi_0Mclg&t=188s) |

None of them divides a sum of N draws by N to reach τ²/N; that takes rows 2 and 3 together, as equation (13.2) does. Khan argues for the rule from ranges and says it is not a proof. StatQuest and Professor Leonard have no video on this rule. Times are read from automatic captions and good to about 30 seconds.
<!-- /video-table -->

| Order | What it covers | Kind | Watch |
|---|---|---|---|

| 2 | The standard error is the standard deviation of means from repeated samples; for a mean it has the formula s / √n | Definition | [StatQuest, The standard error, 7:35 to 8:21](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=XNgt7F6FqDU&t=455s) |

StatQuest states s / √n without deriving it (the derivation is the variance-of-a-sum table), and no row covers correlated draws, such as a Markov chain's, which need a larger standard error. Rows 1 and 2 are read from automatic captions and good to about 30 seconds; row 3 is a chapter boundary from the video's chapter list, so what is said inside was judged from the chapter title and not read.

## The variance of an indicator, p(1 − p)

<!-- video-table: variance-of-an-indicator rev=1 -->
ritvikmath on the variance of a variable that is 1 with probability p and 0 otherwise, in the order to watch:

| Order | What it covers | Kind | Watch |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | A Bernoulli variable takes 1 with probability p and 0 otherwise, so its mean is p | Definition | [ritvikmath, The Bernoulli Distribution, 2:32 to 3:20](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=u-Yezvx33PE&t=152s) |
| 2 | Var(X) = E[X²] − E[X]², and X² equals X for a 0-or-1 variable, so the variance is p − p² = p(1 − p) | Proof | [ritvikmath, The Bernoulli Distribution, 3:20 to 5:05](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=u-Yezvx33PE&t=200s) |
| 3 | n independent indicators added: n copies of p(1 − p) give the binomial variance np(1 − p) | Worked example | [ritvikmath, The Binomial Distribution, 10:09 to 10:54](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=oLnmWcbYnrM&t=609s) |

ritvikmath does not divide by n to get the variance of a proportion, p(1 − p) / n; that is the scaling rule in the variance-of-a-sum table. Times are read from automatic captions and good to about 30 seconds.
<!-- /video-table -->

| Order | What it covers | Kind | Watch |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Logarithms and exponentials undo each other, so an exponential equation with no common base is solved with a logarithm | Intuition | [Professor Leonard, Precalculus 57, 1:04 to 2:05](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=KezbqAAlOZk&t=64s) |
| 2 | 5e^(0.2x) = 7: divide by 5 to isolate the exponential, rewrite it with ln, divide by 0.2 to reach x = 5 ln(7/5) | Worked example | [Professor Leonard, Precalculus 57, 24:39 to 28:22](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=KezbqAAlOZk&t=1479s) |

Leonard rewrites the exponential as a logarithm instead of taking the logarithm of both sides, which lands on the same line; his exponent is positive, and none of his examples has a 1 − u inside the logarithm. Times are read from automatic captions and good to about 30 seconds.
<!-- /video-table -->

## A maximum where the derivative is zero

<!-- video-table: derivative-zero-at-a-maximum rev=1 -->
Professor Leonard on finding a maximum by setting the derivative to zero, in the order to watch:

| Order | What it covers | Kind | Watch |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | The first derivative test: take the derivative, set it to zero for the critical numbers, then test its sign on each side; climbing then falling is a maximum | Definition | [Professor Leonard, Calculus 1 3.3, 3:59 to 8:52](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=nQ6tOORDQ3I&t=239s) |
| 2 | f(x) = x³ − 3x + 1: f′(x) = 3x² − 3 = 0 gives x = ±1, and the sign test makes x = −1 a maximum of 3 and x = 1 a minimum of −1 | Worked example | [Professor Leonard, Calculus 1 3.3, 10:44 to 16:29](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=nQ6tOORDQ3I&t=644s) |

Leonard decides maximum or minimum by the sign change of f′; a quadratic with negative curvature, as in Example 3, is his second derivative test (Calculus 1 3.4), which is not linked here. He does not cover maximising a logarithm in place of the function. Times are read from automatic captions and good to about 30 seconds.
<!-- /video-table -->

## Importance sampling

<!-- video-table: importance-sampling rev=1 -->
DataMListic, then Mutual Information from the open web, on importance sampling, in the order to watch:

| Order | What it covers | Kind | Watch |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | A standard normal beyond four standard deviations happens about 3 times in 100,000, so plain draws almost never land there; draw from a q centred on the tail instead | Intuition | [DataMListic, Importance Sampling, 0:00 to 0:49](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=WV01_ekZr7c&t=0s) |
| 2 | Each draw carries the weight p(x) / q(x): multiplying and dividing by q turns the expectation under p into one under q | Proof | [DataMListic, Importance Sampling, 0:49 to 1:36](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=WV01_ekZr7c&t=49s) |
| 3 | A q that misses where f times p is large gives a few huge weights; the best q is proportional to the size of f times p; 200 plain draws hit the tail zero times where 30 weighted ones are enough | Intuition | [DataMListic, Importance Sampling, 1:36 to 2:38](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=WV01_ekZr7c&t=96s) |
| 4 | An importance sampling example (from the chapter title) | Worked example, open web | [Mutual Information, Importance Sampling, 9:00 to 11:40](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=C3p2wI4RAi8&t=540s) |

Neither estimates a ratio of normalising constants, which is what annealed importance sampling's weights do. The Synthetic Mind's short on importance sampling has neither readable captions nor a chapter list, so it is not linked. Rows 1 to 3 are read from automatic captions and good to about 30 seconds; row 4 is a chapter boundary from the video's chapter list, so what is said inside was judged from the chapter title and not read.
<!-- /video-table -->

## A Gaussian's mean and variance

<!-- video-table: gaussian-mean-and-variance rev=1 -->
ritvikmath on the Gaussian's two parameters, the mean that places the bump and the variance that sets its width, in the order to watch:

| Order | What it covers | Kind | Watch |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | X is normal with parameters μ and σ² when its density is 1/(σ√(2π)) · e^(−(x − μ)²/(2σ²)) on the whole line, with σ² positive and μ any real number | Definition | [ritvikmath, The Normal Distribution, 0:21 to 1:42](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=qpryHt2zBKs&t=21s) |
| 2 | With μ = −1 and σ = 1 the bump sits at −1; changing σ² to 2 leaves it at −1 and spreads it wider | Worked example | [ritvikmath, The Normal Distribution, 9:57 to 10:59](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=qpryHt2zBKs&t=597s) |
| 3 | E[X] = μ: substitute u = (x − μ)/σ, the odd part integrates to 0, and the Gaussian integral squared in polar coordinates gives 2π | Proof | [ritvikmath, The Normal Distribution, 1:42 to 8:36](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=qpryHt2zBKs&t=102s) |
| 4 | Var(X) = E[X²] − μ² = σ², with the new integral done by parts | Proof | [ritvikmath, The Normal Distribution, 8:36 to 9:57](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=qpryHt2zBKs&t=516s) |

ritvikmath never evaluates the density's height at a point, draws the change of width only for σ² = 2, and reads the variance proof off a page he prepared rather than working it live. Times are read from the video's automatic captions and are good to about 30 seconds.
<!-- /video-table -->
